From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F22\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because… I don’t know, it’s gotten to the point where I can’t really function normally around people the way I used to. I keep seeing this droop, this unevenness around my mouth and my jaw—especially in photos, but also in mirrors, windows, even on my phone screen when it goes dark. It feels like it’s the first thing anyone would notice.\n\nPeople tell me I look fine, or that they don’t see what I’m talking about, but that almost makes it worse because I feel like they’re just being polite. To me it’s very obvious. Like one side is lower or heavier, and then my whole face looks wrong.\n\nAnd it’s starting to affect work. I’m an architect, so I have to meet with clients and give presentations, and I’ve started avoiding them when I can. I’ll make excuses or try to have someone else speak, because I can’t stop thinking they’re staring at my mouth or jaw instead of listening to me. So… that’s why I came in. It’s taking over more than I want to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think that maybe your mouth and jaw actually look normal, and it's just your perception of them that's off?\n\nPatient: I mean… logically, I suppose I can understand that maybe other people don’t see it the way I do. They tell me I look fine, they say my mouth and jaw look normal, or that I’m exaggerating it. But when I look—especially in a photo, or even just a reflection in a window—it doesn’t feel like a question. It feels obvious. Like one side is drooping or heavier, and then I can’t see anything else.\n\nSo yes, there’s a small part of me that wonders if my perception is somehow… off, because otherwise why would everyone keep saying they don’t notice it? But that thought doesn’t really stick. The moment I see my face again, it’s right there, and I feel embarrassed, almost exposed. It’s gotten to where I avoid presentations or meeting clients if I can, because I’m sure they’re looking at my mouth and jaw instead of listening to what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your mouth and jaw actually are uneven, even if other people don't see it that way?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do think it’s possible. Honestly, most of the time I think it’s more than possible — it feels like it’s just true. I know people say they don’t see it, or they say, “No, your face looks fine,” but when I look at myself, especially in photos or any kind of reflection, I can’t stop seeing that droop around my mouth and jaw. One side just looks heavier or lower to me, and once I notice it, it’s all I can focus on.\n\nIt’s not like I’m looking for something tiny on purpose. It just jumps out at me. And then I start thinking, well, maybe other people are just being kind, or maybe they don’t want to embarrass me by admitting it. Because to me it feels so obvious that I don’t understand how they wouldn’t see it.\n\nAnd it’s gotten bad enough that I’ve started avoiding presentations and clients when I can, because I’m sitting there imagining they’re looking at my mouth instead of listening to what I’m saying. So even if other people say they don’t notice it, I still feel like the unevenness is there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any chance at all that you might be mistaken about the unevenness of your mouth and jaw?\n\nPatient: I mean… there’s maybe a tiny chance, yes. I can say that because I’m sitting here and I know people keep telling me they don’t see it. They’ll say my mouth looks normal, or my jaw isn’t uneven, and I understand that, logically, that should mean something.\n\nBut when I actually see myself — in a photo, or a reflection, or even just my phone screen when it goes dark — it’s like all of that disappears. I can’t stop seeing this droop around my mouth and jaw. It looks so obvious to me, like one side is lower or heavier, and then I can’t look at my face as a whole anymore. That one thing takes over.\n\nSo I suppose I might be mistaken in the sense that other people don’t seem to react to it the way I do. But it doesn’t feel like I’m mistaken. It feels real enough that I’ve started avoiding presentations and clients, because I’m worried they’re noticing it too and just not saying anything.\n\nDoctor: Does your perception of the unevenness around your mouth and jaw ever change, or does it always look the same to you?\n\nPatient: It changes a bit, yes, but not in a reassuring way. I mean, it’s always there to me — that’s the problem. I can’t really remember the last time I looked at a photo or caught my reflection and didn’t immediately go to my mouth and jaw. But some moments it looks… worse, I guess. Like in certain lighting, or in a photo taken from the wrong angle, it looks like the droop is much more obvious, like one side of my mouth is pulling down or my jaw is sitting unevenly.\n\nAnd then other times it might look slightly less severe, but I still see it. It doesn’t disappear. Even if someone says, “You look fine,” I’ll look again and think, no, it’s right there, how can they not see that? So it isn’t that one day I think I look normal and the next day I don’t. It’s more like the intensity changes, but the unevenness is always the first thing I notice.\n\nThat’s part of why I’m here, honestly. I feel like I can’t stop seeing it in every photo and reflection, and it’s gotten to where I’m avoiding presentations and clients because I’m afraid they’re focused on my mouth and jaw too.\n\nDoctor: Do you think anyone else actually sees the unevenness you're describing, or do you believe they're just not telling you?\n\nPatient: I think… I think they probably do see it, at least some of them. Or maybe they see something and just don’t want to say it. That’s the part I get stuck on, because when people tell me I look fine, I don’t really feel reassured. I know they may mean it kindly, but in my head I’m thinking, they’re just trying not to hurt my feelings, or they noticed and decided it would be rude to mention.\n\nBecause to me it’s so obvious. Every photo, every reflection, I go straight to that area around my mouth and jaw — that droop, the unevenness. I can’t just look at my whole face normally anymore. It’s like my eyes lock onto it immediately, and then I start imagining that everyone else’s eyes are going there too.\n\nThat’s why it’s affected work so much. I’m an architect, I’m supposed to be able to speak to clients and do presentations, and instead I’m worrying that they’re staring at my mouth when I talk, or noticing one side of my jaw looks lower. So yes, when they say they don’t see it, part of me thinks they’re just not telling me. I’m here because I can’t seem to get past that feeling.\n\nDoctor: Has there ever been a time when you didn't notice the unevenness around your mouth and jaw?\n\nPatient: Honestly… not really, not anymore. I mean, I’m sure there must have been a time before I was so focused on it, because I didn’t always live like this, checking every photo and reflection. But lately, I can’t remember looking at myself without immediately going to that area — my mouth, my jaw, that droop or unevenness.\n\nEven if it’s just a quick reflection in a window or my phone screen when it’s dark, my eyes go straight there. And in photos it’s worse, because then I can zoom in or stare at it, and it feels so obvious that I don’t understand how other people can say they don’t see anything. I know they tell me I look fine, but it doesn’t land that way for me.\n\nSo if you mean recently, no — I don’t think there’s really a time when I don’t notice it. It’s like it’s always the first thing I see, and it’s gotten to the point where I avoid presentations and clients because I’m worried they’re seeing exactly what I’m seeing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your perception of the unevenness is not based in reality?\n\nPatient: I mean… I know that’s what you’re asking me to consider, and part of me understands why you’d ask it. But it’s very hard for me to believe it’s not based in reality, because when I look at myself it feels so obvious. I see this droop, this unevenness around my mouth and jaw, and my eyes go straight to it every single time.\n\nIt’s not like I’m looking for something vague and then convincing myself. It feels like it’s right there in every photo, every reflection, even when I catch myself quickly in a window or on my phone screen. And when people say I look fine, I want to believe them, but I don’t really feel convinced. I start thinking maybe they’re being polite, or maybe they don’t want to embarrass me.\n\nSo… possible? I suppose I can say it’s possible because I’m here, and because this has gotten out of hand. I’ve started avoiding presentations and clients because I’m so worried they’re seeing my mouth and jaw the way I see them. But emotionally, no, it doesn’t feel unreal to me. It feels painfully real.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any situation or proof that could convince you that the unevenness around your mouth and jaw isn't actually there?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I wish I could say yes, because that would mean there’s some way out of this, but when I imagine it, I’m not sure what would actually convince me.\n\nPeople have told me I look fine. They’ve said they don’t notice anything, or that my face looks normal, and for a few seconds maybe I try to take that in. But then I’ll see myself in a photo or catch my reflection somewhere, and immediately I’m back to seeing the droop around my mouth, the unevenness in my jaw. It’s like whatever reassurance I got just disappears.\n\nMaybe if there were some kind of objective measurement or something, I’d want to believe it. But even then, I’m afraid I’d think, well, maybe the lighting was different, or maybe they measured the wrong thing, or maybe it only shows when I’m talking. That’s how stuck I feel with it.\n\nI’m here because I really can’t stop seeing it. Every photo, every reflection, that’s where my eyes go first. And it feels so obvious to me that even when other people say I look fine, I still end up avoiding presentations and clients because I’m convinced they’re going to notice my mouth and jaw the way I do. So… I want there to be proof that would help, but right now I honestly don’t know what would be strong enough.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself unable to stop thinking about the unevenness of your mouth and jaw, even when you're trying to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: Yes. Constantly, honestly. Even when I’m trying to focus on work or listen to someone, part of my mind is still on my mouth and jaw — like, how it looks, whether the droop is showing, whether the unevenness is obvious from whatever angle they’re seeing me.\n\nIt’s worst with photos and reflections, because once I see it, I can’t unsee it. My eyes go straight there every time, and then it kind of stays with me afterward. Even if someone tells me I look fine, I keep replaying it in my head and thinking, “No, it’s there, it’s obvious.” It feels like I’m fighting with what they’re saying versus what I’m seeing.\n\nAnd it’s gotten to where it interferes with things I should be able to just do. Presentations, clients — I avoid them when I can, because I’m so preoccupied with the idea that they’ll be looking at that part of my face instead of listening to me. I know that probably sounds excessive, but in the moment it feels impossible to put it aside.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about your mouth and jaw the only thing you worry about when it comes to your appearance, or are there other aspects of how you look that also bother you?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly — really, almost entirely — the mouth and jaw. That’s the thing I can’t get away from. I don’t sit there cataloging every part of my face or body in the same way. It’s this droop, this unevenness around my mouth and jaw, that just pulls my attention over and over.\n\nLike, I can look at a photo and maybe someone else would see the whole picture, but I don’t. My eyes go straight to that area. Same with reflections — bathroom mirror, car window, phone screen, anything. I immediately check whether one side looks lower or whether my jaw looks off. And then once I see it, I feel like it’s all anyone else could possibly see too.\n\nPeople tell me I look fine, and I know I’ve said that, but it doesn’t really settle it for me. It might calm me down for a moment, but then I see myself again and it feels obvious all over again. That’s why I’m here, really. It’s not that I have a long list of appearance things I’m equally worried about — it’s that this one thing has taken over so much that I’ve started avoiding presentations and meeting with clients because I’m convinced they’ll notice my mouth and jaw instead of listening to what I’m saying.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you think there's any situation or proof that could convince you that the unevenness around your mouth and jaw isn't actually there?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I wish I could say yes, because that would mean there’s some way out of this, but when I imagine it, I’m not sure what would actually convince me.\n\nPeople have told me I look fine. They’ve said they don’t notice anything, or that my face looks normal, and for a few seconds maybe I try to take that in. But then I’ll see myself in a photo or catch my reflection somewhere, and immediately I’m back to seeing the droop around my mouth, the unevenness in my jaw. It’s like whatever reassurance I got just disappears.\n\nMaybe if there were some kind of objective measurement or something, I’d want to believe it. But even then, I’m afraid I’d think, well, maybe the lighting was different, or maybe they measured the wrong thing, or maybe it only shows when I’m talking. That’s how stuck I feel with it.\n\nI’m here because I really can’t stop seeing it. Every photo, every reflection, that’s where my eyes go first. And it feels so obvious to me that even when other people say I look fine, I still end up avoiding presentations and clients because I’m convinced they’re going to notice my mouth and jaw the way I do. So… I want there to be proof that would help, but right now I honestly don’t know what would be strong enough.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself unable to stop thinking about the unevenness of your mouth and jaw, even when you're trying to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: Yes. Constantly, honestly. Even when I’m trying to focus on work or listen to someone, part of my mind is still on my mouth and jaw — like, how it looks, whether the droop is showing, whether the unevenness is obvious from whatever angle they’re seeing me.\n\nIt’s worst with photos and reflections, because once I see it, I can’t unsee it. My eyes go straight there every time, and then it kind of stays with me afterward. Even if someone tells me I look fine, I keep replaying it in my head and thinking, “No, it’s there, it’s obvious.” It feels like I’m fighting with what they’re saying versus what I’m seeing.\n\nAnd it’s gotten to where it interferes with things I should be able to just do. Presentations, clients — I avoid them when I can, because I’m so preoccupied with the idea that they’ll be looking at that part of my face instead of listening to me. I know that probably sounds excessive, but in the moment it feels impossible to put it aside.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about your mouth and jaw the only thing you worry about when it comes to your appearance, or are there other aspects of how you look that also bother you?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly — really, almost entirely — the mouth and jaw. That’s the thing I can’t get away from. I don’t sit there cataloging every part of my face or body in the same way. It’s this droop, this unevenness around my mouth and jaw, that just pulls my attention over and over.\n\nLike, I can look at a photo and maybe someone else would see the whole picture, but I don’t. My eyes go straight to that area. Same with reflections — bathroom mirror, car window, phone screen, anything. I immediately check whether one side looks lower or whether my jaw looks off. And then once I see it, I feel like it’s all anyone else could possibly see too.\n\nPeople tell me I look fine, and I know I’ve said that, but it doesn’t really settle it for me. It might calm me down for a moment, but then I see myself again and it feels obvious all over again. That’s why I’m here, really. It’s not that I have a long list of appearance things I’m equally worried about — it’s that this one thing has taken over so much that I’ve started avoiding presentations and meeting with clients because I’m convinced they’ll notice my mouth and jaw instead of listening to what I’m saying.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think your perception of the unevenness could be considered an unreasonable or unrealistic concern by others?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 54 / architect / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Preoccupation_with_Appearance\nmanifestation: \"I’m here because I can’t stop seeing this droop and unevenness around my mouth and jaw in every photo and reflection, and even when people tell me I look fine, it feels so obvious that I’ve started avoiding presentations and clients.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I suppose… yes, I can see that other people might think it’s unreasonable or unrealistic. I mean, people have told me over and over that I look fine, that they don’t see what I’m talking about, or that it’s not noticeable. So I understand, in a logical way, that they might look at me and think I’m making too much of it.

But it doesn’t feel unrealistic to me when I see it. That’s the problem. In every photo, every reflection, my eyes go straight to my mouth and jaw, and I see this droop, this unevenness, like one side isn’t sitting right. And once I see it, it feels so obvious that I can’t really believe other people aren’t seeing it too.

So yes, I can imagine someone else saying, “This isn’t as bad as you think,” or “You’re focused on something minor,” but inside it doesn’t feel minor at all. It feels like it’s right there on my face, and it’s gotten to the point where I’ve avoided presentations and clients because I’m worried they’ll be looking at that instead of listening to me. So… I know others may think my concern is excessive, but I still can’t stop seeing it.

---------- turn 12 | doctor:inference | doctor.build_inference_prompt() | model=meta-llama/Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct ----------
========== INPUT [doctor] ==========
[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F22\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because… I don’t know, it’s gotten to the point where I can’t really function normally around people the way I used to. I keep seeing this droop, this unevenness around my mouth and my jaw—especially in photos, but also in mirrors, windows, even on my phone screen when it goes dark. It feels like it’s the first thing anyone would notice.\n\nPeople tell me I look fine, or that they don’t see what I’m talking about, but that almost makes it worse because I feel like they’re just being polite. To me it’s very obvious. Like one side is lower or heavier, and then my whole face looks wrong.\n\nAnd it’s starting to affect work. I’m an architect, so I have to meet with clients and give presentations, and I’ve started avoiding them when I can. I’ll make excuses or try to have someone else speak, because I can’t stop thinking they’re staring at my mouth or jaw instead of listening to me. So… that’s why I came in. It’s taking over more than I want to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think that maybe your mouth and jaw actually look normal, and it's just your perception of them that's off?\n\nPatient: I mean… logically, I suppose I can understand that maybe other people don’t see it the way I do. They tell me I look fine, they say my mouth and jaw look normal, or that I’m exaggerating it. But when I look—especially in a photo, or even just a reflection in a window—it doesn’t feel like a question. It feels obvious. Like one side is drooping or heavier, and then I can’t see anything else.\n\nSo yes, there’s a small part of me that wonders if my perception is somehow… off, because otherwise why would everyone keep saying they don’t notice it? But that thought doesn’t really stick. The moment I see my face again, it’s right there, and I feel embarrassed, almost exposed. It’s gotten to where I avoid presentations or meeting clients if I can, because I’m sure they’re looking at my mouth and jaw instead of listening to what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your mouth and jaw actually are uneven, even if other people don't see it that way?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do think it’s possible. Honestly, most of the time I think it’s more than possible — it feels like it’s just true. I know people say they don’t see it, or they say, “No, your face looks fine,” but when I look at myself, especially in photos or any kind of reflection, I can’t stop seeing that droop around my mouth and jaw. One side just looks heavier or lower to me, and once I notice it, it’s all I can focus on.\n\nIt’s not like I’m looking for something tiny on purpose. It just jumps out at me. And then I start thinking, well, maybe other people are just being kind, or maybe they don’t want to embarrass me by admitting it. Because to me it feels so obvious that I don’t understand how they wouldn’t see it.\n\nAnd it’s gotten bad enough that I’ve started avoiding presentations and clients when I can, because I’m sitting there imagining they’re looking at my mouth instead of listening to what I’m saying. So even if other people say they don’t notice it, I still feel like the unevenness is there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any chance at all that you might be mistaken about the unevenness of your mouth and jaw?\n\nPatient: I mean… there’s maybe a tiny chance, yes. I can say that because I’m sitting here and I know people keep telling me they don’t see it. They’ll say my mouth looks normal, or my jaw isn’t uneven, and I understand that, logically, that should mean something.\n\nBut when I actually see myself — in a photo, or a reflection, or even just my phone screen when it goes dark — it’s like all of that disappears. I can’t stop seeing this droop around my mouth and jaw. It looks so obvious to me, like one side is lower or heavier, and then I can’t look at my face as a whole anymore. That one thing takes over.\n\nSo I suppose I might be mistaken in the sense that other people don’t seem to react to it the way I do. But it doesn’t feel like I’m mistaken. It feels real enough that I’ve started avoiding presentations and clients, because I’m worried they’re noticing it too and just not saying anything.\n\nDoctor: Does your perception of the unevenness around your mouth and jaw ever change, or does it always look the same to you?\n\nPatient: It changes a bit, yes, but not in a reassuring way. I mean, it’s always there to me — that’s the problem. I can’t really remember the last time I looked at a photo or caught my reflection and didn’t immediately go to my mouth and jaw. But some moments it looks… worse, I guess. Like in certain lighting, or in a photo taken from the wrong angle, it looks like the droop is much more obvious, like one side of my mouth is pulling down or my jaw is sitting unevenly.\n\nAnd then other times it might look slightly less severe, but I still see it. It doesn’t disappear. Even if someone says, “You look fine,” I’ll look again and think, no, it’s right there, how can they not see that? So it isn’t that one day I think I look normal and the next day I don’t. It’s more like the intensity changes, but the unevenness is always the first thing I notice.\n\nThat’s part of why I’m here, honestly. I feel like I can’t stop seeing it in every photo and reflection, and it’s gotten to where I’m avoiding presentations and clients because I’m afraid they’re focused on my mouth and jaw too.\n\nDoctor: Do you think anyone else actually sees the unevenness you're describing, or do you believe they're just not telling you?\n\nPatient: I think… I think they probably do see it, at least some of them. Or maybe they see something and just don’t want to say it. That’s the part I get stuck on, because when people tell me I look fine, I don’t really feel reassured. I know they may mean it kindly, but in my head I’m thinking, they’re just trying not to hurt my feelings, or they noticed and decided it would be rude to mention.\n\nBecause to me it’s so obvious. Every photo, every reflection, I go straight to that area around my mouth and jaw — that droop, the unevenness. I can’t just look at my whole face normally anymore. It’s like my eyes lock onto it immediately, and then I start imagining that everyone else’s eyes are going there too.\n\nThat’s why it’s affected work so much. I’m an architect, I’m supposed to be able to speak to clients and do presentations, and instead I’m worrying that they’re staring at my mouth when I talk, or noticing one side of my jaw looks lower. So yes, when they say they don’t see it, part of me thinks they’re just not telling me. I’m here because I can’t seem to get past that feeling.\n\nDoctor: Has there ever been a time when you didn't notice the unevenness around your mouth and jaw?\n\nPatient: Honestly… not really, not anymore. I mean, I’m sure there must have been a time before I was so focused on it, because I didn’t always live like this, checking every photo and reflection. But lately, I can’t remember looking at myself without immediately going to that area — my mouth, my jaw, that droop or unevenness.\n\nEven if it’s just a quick reflection in a window or my phone screen when it’s dark, my eyes go straight there. And in photos it’s worse, because then I can zoom in or stare at it, and it feels so obvious that I don’t understand how other people can say they don’t see anything. I know they tell me I look fine, but it doesn’t land that way for me.\n\nSo if you mean recently, no — I don’t think there’s really a time when I don’t notice it. It’s like it’s always the first thing I see, and it’s gotten to the point where I avoid presentations and clients because I’m worried they’re seeing exactly what I’m seeing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your perception of the unevenness is not based in reality?\n\nPatient: I mean… I know that’s what you’re asking me to consider, and part of me understands why you’d ask it. But it’s very hard for me to believe it’s not based in reality, because when I look at myself it feels so obvious. I see this droop, this unevenness around my mouth and jaw, and my eyes go straight to it every single time.\n\nIt’s not like I’m looking for something vague and then convincing myself. It feels like it’s right there in every photo, every reflection, even when I catch myself quickly in a window or on my phone screen. And when people say I look fine, I want to believe them, but I don’t really feel convinced. I start thinking maybe they’re being polite, or maybe they don’t want to embarrass me.\n\nSo… possible? I suppose I can say it’s possible because I’m here, and because this has gotten out of hand. I’ve started avoiding presentations and clients because I’m so worried they’re seeing my mouth and jaw the way I see them. But emotionally, no, it doesn’t feel unreal to me. It feels painfully real.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any situation or proof that could convince you that the unevenness around your mouth and jaw isn't actually there?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I wish I could say yes, because that would mean there’s some way out of this, but when I imagine it, I’m not sure what would actually convince me.\n\nPeople have told me I look fine. They’ve said they don’t notice anything, or that my face looks normal, and for a few seconds maybe I try to take that in. But then I’ll see myself in a photo or catch my reflection somewhere, and immediately I’m back to seeing the droop around my mouth, the unevenness in my jaw. It’s like whatever reassurance I got just disappears.\n\nMaybe if there were some kind of objective measurement or something, I’d want to believe it. But even then, I’m afraid I’d think, well, maybe the lighting was different, or maybe they measured the wrong thing, or maybe it only shows when I’m talking. That’s how stuck I feel with it.\n\nI’m here because I really can’t stop seeing it. Every photo, every reflection, that’s where my eyes go first. And it feels so obvious to me that even when other people say I look fine, I still end up avoiding presentations and clients because I’m convinced they’re going to notice my mouth and jaw the way I do. So… I want there to be proof that would help, but right now I honestly don’t know what would be strong enough.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself unable to stop thinking about the unevenness of your mouth and jaw, even when you're trying to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: Yes. Constantly, honestly. Even when I’m trying to focus on work or listen to someone, part of my mind is still on my mouth and jaw — like, how it looks, whether the droop is showing, whether the unevenness is obvious from whatever angle they’re seeing me.\n\nIt’s worst with photos and reflections, because once I see it, I can’t unsee it. My eyes go straight there every time, and then it kind of stays with me afterward. Even if someone tells me I look fine, I keep replaying it in my head and thinking, “No, it’s there, it’s obvious.” It feels like I’m fighting with what they’re saying versus what I’m seeing.\n\nAnd it’s gotten to where it interferes with things I should be able to just do. Presentations, clients — I avoid them when I can, because I’m so preoccupied with the idea that they’ll be looking at that part of my face instead of listening to me. I know that probably sounds excessive, but in the moment it feels impossible to put it aside.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about your mouth and jaw the only thing you worry about when it comes to your appearance, or are there other aspects of how you look that also bother you?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly — really, almost entirely — the mouth and jaw. That’s the thing I can’t get away from. I don’t sit there cataloging every part of my face or body in the same way. It’s this droop, this unevenness around my mouth and jaw, that just pulls my attention over and over.\n\nLike, I can look at a photo and maybe someone else would see the whole picture, but I don’t. My eyes go straight to that area. Same with reflections — bathroom mirror, car window, phone screen, anything. I immediately check whether one side looks lower or whether my jaw looks off. And then once I see it, I feel like it’s all anyone else could possibly see too.\n\nPeople tell me I look fine, and I know I’ve said that, but it doesn’t really settle it for me. It might calm me down for a moment, but then I see myself again and it feels obvious all over again. That’s why I’m here, really. It’s not that I have a long list of appearance things I’m equally worried about — it’s that this one thing has taken over so much that I’ve started avoiding presentations and meeting with clients because I’m convinced they’ll notice my mouth and jaw instead of listening to what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your perception of the unevenness could be considered an unreasonable or unrealistic concern by others?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, I can see that other people might think it’s unreasonable or unrealistic. I mean, people have told me over and over that I look fine, that they don’t see what I’m talking about, or that it’s not noticeable. So I understand, in a logical way, that they might look at me and think I’m making too much of it.\n\nBut it doesn’t feel unrealistic to me when I see it. That’s the problem. In every photo, every reflection, my eyes go straight to my mouth and jaw, and I see this droop, this unevenness, like one side isn’t sitting right. And once I see it, it feels so obvious that I can’t really believe other people aren’t seeing it too.\n\nSo yes, I can imagine someone else saying, “This isn’t as bad as you think,” or “You’re focused on something minor,” but inside it doesn’t feel minor at all. It feels like it’s right there on my face, and it’s gotten to the point where I’ve avoided presentations and clients because I’m worried they’ll be looking at that instead of listening to me. So… I know others may think my concern is excessive, but I still can’t stop seeing it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I’m here because I can’t seem to get past that feeling.\n\nDoctor: Has there ever been a time when you didn't notice the unevenness around your mouth and jaw?\n\nPatient: Honestly… not really, not anymore. I mean, I’m sure there must have been a time before I was so focused on it, because I didn’t always live like this, checking every photo and reflection. But lately, I can’t remember looking at myself without immediately going to that area — my mouth, my jaw, that droop or unevenness.\n\nEven if it’s just a quick reflection in a window or my phone screen when it’s dark, my eyes go straight there. And in photos it’s worse, because then I can zoom in or stare at it, and it feels so obvious that I don’t understand how other people can say they don’t see anything. I know they tell me I look fine, but it doesn’t land that way for me.\n\nSo if you mean recently, no — I don’t think there’s really a time when I don’t notice it. It’s like it’s always the first thing I see, and it’s gotten to the point where I avoid presentations and clients because I’m worried they’re seeing exactly what I’m seeing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your perception of the unevenness is not based in reality?\n\nPatient: I mean… I know that’s what you’re asking me to consider, and part of me understands why you’d ask it. But it’s very hard for me to believe it’s not based in reality, because when I look at myself it feels so obvious. I see this droop, this unevenness around my mouth and jaw, and my eyes go straight to it every single time.\n\nIt’s not like I’m looking for something vague and then convincing myself. It feels like it’s right there in every photo, every reflection, even when I catch myself quickly in a window or on my phone screen. And when people say I look fine, I want to believe them, but I don’t really feel convinced. I start thinking maybe they’re being polite, or maybe they don’t want to embarrass me.\n\nSo… possible? I suppose I can say it’s possible because I’m here, and because this has gotten out of hand. I’ve started avoiding presentations and clients because I’m so worried they’re seeing my mouth and jaw the way I see them. But emotionally, no, it doesn’t feel unreal to me. It feels painfully real.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any situation or proof that could convince you that the unevenness around your mouth and jaw isn't actually there?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I wish I could say yes, because that would mean there’s some way out of this, but when I imagine it, I’m not sure what would actually convince me.\n\nPeople have told me I look fine. They’ve said they don’t notice anything, or that my face looks normal, and for a few seconds maybe I try to take that in. But then I’ll see myself in a photo or catch my reflection somewhere, and immediately I’m back to seeing the droop around my mouth, the unevenness in my jaw. It’s like whatever reassurance I got just disappears.\n\nMaybe if there were some kind of objective measurement or something, I’d want to believe it. But even then, I’m afraid I’d think, well, maybe the lighting was different, or maybe they measured the wrong thing, or maybe it only shows when I’m talking. That’s how stuck I feel with it.\n\nI’m here because I really can’t stop seeing it. Every photo, every reflection, that’s where my eyes go first. And it feels so obvious to me that even when other people say I look fine, I still end up avoiding presentations and clients because I’m convinced they’re going to notice my mouth and jaw the way I do. So… I want there to be proof that would help, but right now I honestly don’t know what would be strong enough.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself unable to stop thinking about the unevenness of your mouth and jaw, even when you're trying to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: Yes. Constantly, honestly. Even when I’m trying to focus on work or listen to someone, part of my mind is still on my mouth and jaw — like, how it looks, whether the droop is showing, whether the unevenness is obvious from whatever angle they’re seeing me.\n\nIt’s worst with photos and reflections, because once I see it, I can’t unsee it. My eyes go straight there every time, and then it kind of stays with me afterward. Even if someone tells me I look fine, I keep replaying it in my head and thinking, “No, it’s there, it’s obvious.” It feels like I’m fighting with what they’re saying versus what I’m seeing.\n\nAnd it’s gotten to where it interferes with things I should be able to just do. Presentations, clients — I avoid them when I can, because I’m so preoccupied with the idea that they’ll be looking at that part of my face instead of listening to me. I know that probably sounds excessive, but in the moment it feels impossible to put it aside.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about your mouth and jaw the only thing you worry about when it comes to your appearance, or are there other aspects of how you look that also bother you?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly — really, almost entirely — the mouth and jaw. That’s the thing I can’t get away from. I don’t sit there cataloging every part of my face or body in the same way. It’s this droop, this unevenness around my mouth and jaw, that just pulls my attention over and over.\n\nLike, I can look at a photo and maybe someone else would see the whole picture, but I don’t. My eyes go straight to that area. Same with reflections — bathroom mirror, car window, phone screen, anything. I immediately check whether one side looks lower or whether my jaw looks off. And then once I see it, I feel like it’s all anyone else could possibly see too.\n\nPeople tell me I look fine, and I know I’ve said that, but it doesn’t really settle it for me. It might calm me down for a moment, but then I see myself again and it feels obvious all over again. That’s why I’m here, really. It’s not that I have a long list of appearance things I’m equally worried about — it’s that this one thing has taken over so much that I’ve started avoiding presentations and meeting with clients because I’m convinced they’ll notice my mouth and jaw instead of listening to what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your perception of the unevenness could be considered an unreasonable or unrealistic concern by others?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, I can see that other people might think it’s unreasonable or unrealistic. I mean, people have told me over and over that I look fine, that they don’t see what I’m talking about, or that it’s not noticeable. So I understand, in a logical way, that they might look at me and think I’m making too much of it.\n\nBut it doesn’t feel unrealistic to me when I see it. That’s the problem. In every photo, every reflection, my eyes go straight to my mouth and jaw, and I see this droop, this unevenness, like one side isn’t sitting right. And once I see it, it feels so obvious that I can’t really believe other people aren’t seeing it too.\n\nSo yes, I can imagine someone else saying, “This isn’t as bad as you think,” or “You’re focused on something minor,” but inside it doesn’t feel minor at all. It feels like it’s right there on my face, and it’s gotten to the point where I’ve avoided presentations and clients because I’m worried they’ll be looking at that instead of listening to me. So… I know others may think my concern is excessive, but I still can’t stop seeing it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Not really, no. I mean… I wish I could say it goes away for stretches, but it doesn’t feel like it does. It may quiet down for a little while if I’m completely distracted, but then I’ll catch myself in a reflection or see a photo and my eyes go straight back to my mouth and jaw.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because… I don’t know, it’s gotten to the point where I can’t really function normally around people the way I used to. I keep seeing this droop, this unevenness around my mouth and my jaw—especially in photos, but also in mirrors, windows, even on my phone screen when it goes dark. It feels like it’s the first thing anyone would notice.\n\nPeople tell me I look fine, or that they don’t see what I’m talking about, but that almost makes it worse because I feel like they’re just being polite. To me it’s very obvious. Like one side is lower or heavier, and then my whole face looks wrong.\n\nAnd it’s starting to affect work. I’m an architect, so I have to meet with clients and give presentations, and I’ve started avoiding them when I can. I’ll make excuses or try to have someone else speak, because I can’t stop thinking they’re staring at my mouth or jaw instead of listening to me. So… that’s why I came in. It’s taking over more than I want to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think that maybe your mouth and jaw actually look normal, and it's just your perception of them that's off?\n\nPatient: I mean… logically, I suppose I can understand that maybe other people don’t see it the way I do. They tell me I look fine, they say my mouth and jaw look normal, or that I’m exaggerating it. But when I look—especially in a photo, or even just a reflection in a window—it doesn’t feel like a question. It feels obvious. Like one side is drooping or heavier, and then I can’t see anything else.\n\nSo yes, there’s a small part of me that wonders if my perception is somehow… off, because otherwise why would everyone keep saying they don’t notice it? But that thought doesn’t really stick. The moment I see my face again, it’s right there, and I feel embarrassed, almost exposed. It’s gotten to where I avoid presentations or meeting clients if I can, because I’m sure they’re looking at my mouth and jaw instead of listening to what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your mouth and jaw actually are uneven, even if other people don't see it that way?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do think it’s possible. Honestly, most of the time I think it’s more than possible — it feels like it’s just true. I know people say they don’t see it, or they say, “No, your face looks fine,” but when I look at myself, especially in photos or any kind of reflection, I can’t stop seeing that droop around my mouth and jaw. One side just looks heavier or lower to me, and once I notice it, it’s all I can focus on.\n\nIt’s not like I’m looking for something tiny on purpose. It just jumps out at me. And then I start thinking, well, maybe other people are just being kind, or maybe they don’t want to embarrass me by admitting it. Because to me it feels so obvious that I don’t understand how they wouldn’t see it.\n\nAnd it’s gotten bad enough that I’ve started avoiding presentations and clients when I can, because I’m sitting there imagining they’re looking at my mouth instead of listening to what I’m saying. So even if other people say they don’t notice it, I still feel like the unevenness is there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any chance at all that you might be mistaken about the unevenness of your mouth and jaw?\n\nPatient: I mean… there’s maybe a tiny chance, yes. I can say that because I’m sitting here and I know people keep telling me they don’t see it. They’ll say my mouth looks normal, or my jaw isn’t uneven, and I understand that, logically, that should mean something.\n\nBut when I actually see myself — in a photo, or a reflection, or even just my phone screen when it goes dark — it’s like all of that disappears. I can’t stop seeing this droop around my mouth and jaw. It looks so obvious to me, like one side is lower or heavier, and then I can’t look at my face as a whole anymore. That one thing takes over.\n\nSo I suppose I might be mistaken in the sense that other people don’t seem to react to it the way I do. But it doesn’t feel like I’m mistaken. It feels real enough that I’ve started avoiding presentations and clients, because I’m worried they’re noticing it too and just not saying anything.\n\nDoctor: Does your perception of the unevenness around your mouth and jaw ever change, or does it always look the same to you?\n\nPatient: It changes a bit, yes, but not in a reassuring way. I mean, it’s always there to me — that’s the problem. I can’t really remember the last time I looked at a photo or caught my reflection and didn’t immediately go to my mouth and jaw. But some moments it looks… worse, I guess. Like in certain lighting, or in a photo taken from the wrong angle, it looks like the droop is much more obvious, like one side of my mouth is pulling down or my jaw is sitting unevenly.\n\nAnd then other times it might look slightly less severe, but I still see it. It doesn’t disappear. Even if someone says, “You look fine,” I’ll look again and think, no, it’s right there, how can they not see that? So it isn’t that one day I think I look normal and the next day I don’t. It’s more like the intensity changes, but the unevenness is always the first thing I notice.\n\nThat’s part of why I’m here, honestly. I feel like I can’t stop seeing it in every photo and reflection, and it’s gotten to where I’m avoiding presentations and clients because I’m afraid they’re focused on my mouth and jaw too.\n\nDoctor: Do you think anyone else actually sees the unevenness you're describing, or do you believe they're just not telling you?\n\nPatient: I think… I think they probably do see it, at least some of them. Or maybe they see something and just don’t want to say it. That’s the part I get stuck on, because when people tell me I look fine, I don’t really feel reassured. I know they may mean it kindly, but in my head I’m thinking, they’re just trying not to hurt my feelings, or they noticed and decided it would be rude to mention.\n\nBecause to me it’s so obvious. Every photo, every reflection, I go straight to that area around my mouth and jaw — that droop, the unevenness. I can’t just look at my whole face normally anymore. It’s like my eyes lock onto it immediately, and then I start imagining that everyone else’s eyes are going there too.\n\nThat’s why it’s affected work so much. I’m an architect, I’m supposed to be able to speak to clients and do presentations, and instead I’m worrying that they’re staring at my mouth when I talk, or noticing one side of my jaw looks lower. So yes, when they say they don’t see it, part of me thinks they’re just not telling me. I’m here because I can’t seem to get past that feeling.\n\nDoctor: Has there ever been a time when you didn't notice the unevenness around your mouth and jaw?\n\nPatient: Honestly… not really, not anymore. I mean, I’m sure there must have been a time before I was so focused on it, because I didn’t always live like this, checking every photo and reflection. But lately, I can’t remember looking at myself without immediately going to that area — my mouth, my jaw, that droop or unevenness.\n\nEven if it’s just a quick reflection in a window or my phone screen when it’s dark, my eyes go straight there. And in photos it’s worse, because then I can zoom in or stare at it, and it feels so obvious that I don’t understand how other people can say they don’t see anything. I know they tell me I look fine, but it doesn’t land that way for me.\n\nSo if you mean recently, no — I don’t think there’s really a time when I don’t notice it. It’s like it’s always the first thing I see, and it’s gotten to the point where I avoid presentations and clients because I’m worried they’re seeing exactly what I’m seeing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your perception of the unevenness is not based in reality?\n\nPatient: I mean… I know that’s what you’re asking me to consider, and part of me understands why you’d ask it. But it’s very hard for me to believe it’s not based in reality, because when I look at myself it feels so obvious. I see this droop, this unevenness around my mouth and jaw, and my eyes go straight to it every single time.\n\nIt’s not like I’m looking for something vague and then convincing myself. It feels like it’s right there in every photo, every reflection, even when I catch myself quickly in a window or on my phone screen. And when people say I look fine, I want to believe them, but I don’t really feel convinced. I start thinking maybe they’re being polite, or maybe they don’t want to embarrass me.\n\nSo… possible? I suppose I can say it’s possible because I’m here, and because this has gotten out of hand. I’ve started avoiding presentations and clients because I’m so worried they’re seeing my mouth and jaw the way I see them. But emotionally, no, it doesn’t feel unreal to me. It feels painfully real.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any situation or proof that could convince you that the unevenness around your mouth and jaw isn't actually there?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I wish I could say yes, because that would mean there’s some way out of this, but when I imagine it, I’m not sure what would actually convince me.\n\nPeople have told me I look fine. They’ve said they don’t notice anything, or that my face looks normal, and for a few seconds maybe I try to take that in. But then I’ll see myself in a photo or catch my reflection somewhere, and immediately I’m back to seeing the droop around my mouth, the unevenness in my jaw. It’s like whatever reassurance I got just disappears.\n\nMaybe if there were some kind of objective measurement or something, I’d want to believe it. But even then, I’m afraid I’d think, well, maybe the lighting was different, or maybe they measured the wrong thing, or maybe it only shows when I’m talking. That’s how stuck I feel with it.\n\nI’m here because I really can’t stop seeing it. Every photo, every reflection, that’s where my eyes go first. And it feels so obvious to me that even when other people say I look fine, I still end up avoiding presentations and clients because I’m convinced they’re going to notice my mouth and jaw the way I do. So… I want there to be proof that would help, but right now I honestly don’t know what would be strong enough.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself unable to stop thinking about the unevenness of your mouth and jaw, even when you're trying to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: Yes. Constantly, honestly. Even when I’m trying to focus on work or listen to someone, part of my mind is still on my mouth and jaw — like, how it looks, whether the droop is showing, whether the unevenness is obvious from whatever angle they’re seeing me.\n\nIt’s worst with photos and reflections, because once I see it, I can’t unsee it. My eyes go straight there every time, and then it kind of stays with me afterward. Even if someone tells me I look fine, I keep replaying it in my head and thinking, “No, it’s there, it’s obvious.” It feels like I’m fighting with what they’re saying versus what I’m seeing.\n\nAnd it’s gotten to where it interferes with things I should be able to just do. Presentations, clients — I avoid them when I can, because I’m so preoccupied with the idea that they’ll be looking at that part of my face instead of listening to me. I know that probably sounds excessive, but in the moment it feels impossible to put it aside.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about your mouth and jaw the only thing you worry about when it comes to your appearance, or are there other aspects of how you look that also bother you?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly — really, almost entirely — the mouth and jaw. That’s the thing I can’t get away from. I don’t sit there cataloging every part of my face or body in the same way. It’s this droop, this unevenness around my mouth and jaw, that just pulls my attention over and over.\n\nLike, I can look at a photo and maybe someone else would see the whole picture, but I don’t. My eyes go straight to that area. Same with reflections — bathroom mirror, car window, phone screen, anything. I immediately check whether one side looks lower or whether my jaw looks off. And then once I see it, I feel like it’s all anyone else could possibly see too.\n\nPeople tell me I look fine, and I know I’ve said that, but it doesn’t really settle it for me. It might calm me down for a moment, but then I see myself again and it feels obvious all over again. That’s why I’m here, really. It’s not that I have a long list of appearance things I’m equally worried about — it’s that this one thing has taken over so much that I’ve started avoiding presentations and meeting with clients because I’m convinced they’ll notice my mouth and jaw instead of listening to what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your perception of the unevenness could be considered an unreasonable or unrealistic concern by others?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, I can see that other people might think it’s unreasonable or unrealistic. I mean, people have told me over and over that I look fine, that they don’t see what I’m talking about, or that it’s not noticeable. So I understand, in a logical way, that they might look at me and think I’m making too much of it.\n\nBut it doesn’t feel unrealistic to me when I see it. That’s the problem. In every photo, every reflection, my eyes go straight to my mouth and jaw, and I see this droop, this unevenness, like one side isn’t sitting right. And once I see it, it feels so obvious that I can’t really believe other people aren’t seeing it too.\n\nSo yes, I can imagine someone else saying, “This isn’t as bad as you think,” or “You’re focused on something minor,” but inside it doesn’t feel minor at all. It feels like it’s right there on my face, and it’s gotten to the point where I’ve avoided presentations and clients because I’m worried they’ll be looking at that instead of listening to me. So… I know others may think my concern is excessive, but I still can’t stop seeing it.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the unevenness of your mouth and jaw ever gone away for a while, even if it came back later?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… I wish I could say it goes away for stretches, but it doesn’t feel like it does. It may quiet down for a little while if I’m completely distracted, but then I’ll catch myself in a reflection or see a photo and my eyes go straight back to my mouth and jaw.\n\nThat’s really why I’m here — because I can’t stop seeing this droop, this unevenness around my mouth and jaw. Every photo, every reflection, it’s like that’s the only thing I can focus on. And even when people tell me I look fine, or they say they don’t notice anything, it doesn’t last. For a moment I might try to believe them, but then I see myself again and it feels obvious all over again.\n\nSo no, I wouldn’t say it has really gone away. It’s become this constant thing in the background, and sometimes very much in the foreground, to the point where I’ve started avoiding presentations and clients because I’m so worried they’ll be looking at that instead of listening to me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No… I don’t think there’s been a time when I didn’t think about it at all. Maybe if I’m really absorbed in something for a short while, I’m not actively thinking, “my mouth, my jaw,” every second. But it’s never gone in the way you’re asking, like some period where I just forgot about it or looked normal to myself.

It’s always there waiting, if that makes sense. I’ll see myself in a photo or even just catch a reflection in a window, and immediately my attention goes straight to that droop, that unevenness around my mouth and jaw. And then it’s like the whole rest of my face disappears. I can’t see what other people say they see.

Even when people tell me I look fine, I can hear the words, and maybe I try to accept it for a moment, but it doesn’t really take. Because as soon as I see myself again, it feels obvious all over again. That’s why it’s become so hard with work things, like presentations or meeting clients. I start thinking they’re going to be focused on my mouth and jaw instead of what I’m saying. So no, I can’t really say there’s been a period where I didn’t think about it at all.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F22\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because… I don’t know, it’s gotten to the point where I can’t really function normally around people the way I used to. I keep seeing this droop, this unevenness around my mouth and my jaw—especially in photos, but also in mirrors, windows, even on my phone screen when it goes dark. It feels like it’s the first thing anyone would notice.\n\nPeople tell me I look fine, or that they don’t see what I’m talking about, but that almost makes it worse because I feel like they’re just being polite. To me it’s very obvious. Like one side is lower or heavier, and then my whole face looks wrong.\n\nAnd it’s starting to affect work. I’m an architect, so I have to meet with clients and give presentations, and I’ve started avoiding them when I can. I’ll make excuses or try to have someone else speak, because I can’t stop thinking they’re staring at my mouth or jaw instead of listening to me. So… that’s why I came in. It’s taking over more than I want to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think that maybe your mouth and jaw actually look normal, and it's just your perception of them that's off?\n\nPatient: I mean… logically, I suppose I can understand that maybe other people don’t see it the way I do. They tell me I look fine, they say my mouth and jaw look normal, or that I’m exaggerating it. But when I look—especially in a photo, or even just a reflection in a window—it doesn’t feel like a question. It feels obvious. Like one side is drooping or heavier, and then I can’t see anything else.\n\nSo yes, there’s a small part of me that wonders if my perception is somehow… off, because otherwise why would everyone keep saying they don’t notice it? But that thought doesn’t really stick. The moment I see my face again, it’s right there, and I feel embarrassed, almost exposed. It’s gotten to where I avoid presentations or meeting clients if I can, because I’m sure they’re looking at my mouth and jaw instead of listening to what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your mouth and jaw actually are uneven, even if other people don't see it that way?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do think it’s possible. Honestly, most of the time I think it’s more than possible — it feels like it’s just true. I know people say they don’t see it, or they say, “No, your face looks fine,” but when I look at myself, especially in photos or any kind of reflection, I can’t stop seeing that droop around my mouth and jaw. One side just looks heavier or lower to me, and once I notice it, it’s all I can focus on.\n\nIt’s not like I’m looking for something tiny on purpose. It just jumps out at me. And then I start thinking, well, maybe other people are just being kind, or maybe they don’t want to embarrass me by admitting it. Because to me it feels so obvious that I don’t understand how they wouldn’t see it.\n\nAnd it’s gotten bad enough that I’ve started avoiding presentations and clients when I can, because I’m sitting there imagining they’re looking at my mouth instead of listening to what I’m saying. So even if other people say they don’t notice it, I still feel like the unevenness is there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any chance at all that you might be mistaken about the unevenness of your mouth and jaw?\n\nPatient: I mean… there’s maybe a tiny chance, yes. I can say that because I’m sitting here and I know people keep telling me they don’t see it. They’ll say my mouth looks normal, or my jaw isn’t uneven, and I understand that, logically, that should mean something.\n\nBut when I actually see myself — in a photo, or a reflection, or even just my phone screen when it goes dark — it’s like all of that disappears. I can’t stop seeing this droop around my mouth and jaw. It looks so obvious to me, like one side is lower or heavier, and then I can’t look at my face as a whole anymore. That one thing takes over.\n\nSo I suppose I might be mistaken in the sense that other people don’t seem to react to it the way I do. But it doesn’t feel like I’m mistaken. It feels real enough that I’ve started avoiding presentations and clients, because I’m worried they’re noticing it too and just not saying anything.\n\nDoctor: Does your perception of the unevenness around your mouth and jaw ever change, or does it always look the same to you?\n\nPatient: It changes a bit, yes, but not in a reassuring way. I mean, it’s always there to me — that’s the problem. I can’t really remember the last time I looked at a photo or caught my reflection and didn’t immediately go to my mouth and jaw. But some moments it looks… worse, I guess. Like in certain lighting, or in a photo taken from the wrong angle, it looks like the droop is much more obvious, like one side of my mouth is pulling down or my jaw is sitting unevenly.\n\nAnd then other times it might look slightly less severe, but I still see it. It doesn’t disappear. Even if someone says, “You look fine,” I’ll look again and think, no, it’s right there, how can they not see that? So it isn’t that one day I think I look normal and the next day I don’t. It’s more like the intensity changes, but the unevenness is always the first thing I notice.\n\nThat’s part of why I’m here, honestly. I feel like I can’t stop seeing it in every photo and reflection, and it’s gotten to where I’m avoiding presentations and clients because I’m afraid they’re focused on my mouth and jaw too.\n\nDoctor: Do you think anyone else actually sees the unevenness you're describing, or do you believe they're just not telling you?\n\nPatient: I think… I think they probably do see it, at least some of them. Or maybe they see something and just don’t want to say it. That’s the part I get stuck on, because when people tell me I look fine, I don’t really feel reassured. I know they may mean it kindly, but in my head I’m thinking, they’re just trying not to hurt my feelings, or they noticed and decided it would be rude to mention.\n\nBecause to me it’s so obvious. Every photo, every reflection, I go straight to that area around my mouth and jaw — that droop, the unevenness. I can’t just look at my whole face normally anymore. It’s like my eyes lock onto it immediately, and then I start imagining that everyone else’s eyes are going there too.\n\nThat’s why it’s affected work so much. I’m an architect, I’m supposed to be able to speak to clients and do presentations, and instead I’m worrying that they’re staring at my mouth when I talk, or noticing one side of my jaw looks lower. So yes, when they say they don’t see it, part of me thinks they’re just not telling me. I’m here because I can’t seem to get past that feeling.\n\nDoctor: Has there ever been a time when you didn't notice the unevenness around your mouth and jaw?\n\nPatient: Honestly… not really, not anymore. I mean, I’m sure there must have been a time before I was so focused on it, because I didn’t always live like this, checking every photo and reflection. But lately, I can’t remember looking at myself without immediately going to that area — my mouth, my jaw, that droop or unevenness.\n\nEven if it’s just a quick reflection in a window or my phone screen when it’s dark, my eyes go straight there. And in photos it’s worse, because then I can zoom in or stare at it, and it feels so obvious that I don’t understand how other people can say they don’t see anything. I know they tell me I look fine, but it doesn’t land that way for me.\n\nSo if you mean recently, no — I don’t think there’s really a time when I don’t notice it. It’s like it’s always the first thing I see, and it’s gotten to the point where I avoid presentations and clients because I’m worried they’re seeing exactly what I’m seeing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your perception of the unevenness is not based in reality?\n\nPatient: I mean… I know that’s what you’re asking me to consider, and part of me understands why you’d ask it. But it’s very hard for me to believe it’s not based in reality, because when I look at myself it feels so obvious. I see this droop, this unevenness around my mouth and jaw, and my eyes go straight to it every single time.\n\nIt’s not like I’m looking for something vague and then convincing myself. It feels like it’s right there in every photo, every reflection, even when I catch myself quickly in a window or on my phone screen. And when people say I look fine, I want to believe them, but I don’t really feel convinced. I start thinking maybe they’re being polite, or maybe they don’t want to embarrass me.\n\nSo… possible? I suppose I can say it’s possible because I’m here, and because this has gotten out of hand. I’ve started avoiding presentations and clients because I’m so worried they’re seeing my mouth and jaw the way I see them. But emotionally, no, it doesn’t feel unreal to me. It feels painfully real.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any situation or proof that could convince you that the unevenness around your mouth and jaw isn't actually there?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I wish I could say yes, because that would mean there’s some way out of this, but when I imagine it, I’m not sure what would actually convince me.\n\nPeople have told me I look fine. They’ve said they don’t notice anything, or that my face looks normal, and for a few seconds maybe I try to take that in. But then I’ll see myself in a photo or catch my reflection somewhere, and immediately I’m back to seeing the droop around my mouth, the unevenness in my jaw. It’s like whatever reassurance I got just disappears.\n\nMaybe if there were some kind of objective measurement or something, I’d want to believe it. But even then, I’m afraid I’d think, well, maybe the lighting was different, or maybe they measured the wrong thing, or maybe it only shows when I’m talking. That’s how stuck I feel with it.\n\nI’m here because I really can’t stop seeing it. Every photo, every reflection, that’s where my eyes go first. And it feels so obvious to me that even when other people say I look fine, I still end up avoiding presentations and clients because I’m convinced they’re going to notice my mouth and jaw the way I do. So… I want there to be proof that would help, but right now I honestly don’t know what would be strong enough.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself unable to stop thinking about the unevenness of your mouth and jaw, even when you're trying to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: Yes. Constantly, honestly. Even when I’m trying to focus on work or listen to someone, part of my mind is still on my mouth and jaw — like, how it looks, whether the droop is showing, whether the unevenness is obvious from whatever angle they’re seeing me.\n\nIt’s worst with photos and reflections, because once I see it, I can’t unsee it. My eyes go straight there every time, and then it kind of stays with me afterward. Even if someone tells me I look fine, I keep replaying it in my head and thinking, “No, it’s there, it’s obvious.” It feels like I’m fighting with what they’re saying versus what I’m seeing.\n\nAnd it’s gotten to where it interferes with things I should be able to just do.
